| **ATT-3** | Re-mapping sets `user_uuid` to the agency's `AgencyIdentity` UUID, removes `google_analytics_cookies`, keeps `application_url` (the service provider's redirect URI), keeps IP address, user agent, device id and all event-specific fields, and adds `delegation_id` and `actor_issuer`. `subject.session_id` remains the value the service provider supplied in `attempts_api_session_id` (or null), per the published schema, so the agency's joins on that field keep their meaning. |
| **ATT-4** | Nothing is delivered to, and no `AgencyIdentity` is created for, an agency the person declined; `user_uuid` resolution is deferred until consent. |
| **ATT-5** | When consent is skipped because remembered approvals cover the request, ATT-2 runs at the handoff instead, and the `delegated-access-consented` event carries `remembered: true` and the original `consented_at`. |
| **ATT-6** | `login-completed` at the handoff is also delivered to each approved agency, re-mapped; on the remembered path the release happens before that event is recorded so the agency receives it. |
| **ATT-7** | Each exchange emits `delegated-access-token-issued`; each refresh `delegated-access-token-refreshed`, written from the refresh grant after the live token is written; each approval or family revocation `delegated-access-revoked` with a `reason` (`user_revoked`, `sp_disconnected`, `refresh_token_reuse`, `account_suspended`, `account_deleted`, `client_revoked`), a family's end written from `TokenExchangeRefreshToken.revoke_family!` to the one API's recipient. Every delegated event is written through the `TrackerEvents` method its schema is generated from (`AttemptsApi::DelegatedEventWriter` includes `TrackerEvents`). Server-side events carry no IP address, user agent, port or device. `superseded_by_new_consent` is never reported, since a re-approval carries the live tokens over and nothing ended for the agency. |
| **ATT-8** | While the service provider's browser session is live, re-authentication, MFA change, logout, timeout and rate-limit events are also delivered to each approved agency. A recipient enters live fan-out only when the sign-in under way is to the service provider its approvals are for; an advance approval on the account page releases the consent event and the history but does not start forwarding the account session's events (D66). |
| **ATT-9** | Introspection returns `delegation_id` (INT-4) so the agency can join API calls to these events. |
| **ATT-10** | The new event types and the `delegation_id` and `actor_issuer` properties are defined in `app/services/attempts_api/tracker_events.rb` and in the OpenAPI schemas under `docs/attempts-api/schemas/events/` (`DelegationEvents.yml` referenced from `AllEvents.yml`, the four files under `delegated-access/`, the two properties in `shared/EventProperties.yml`). |
| **ATT-11** | The delegation context and the event buffer live at the Rails session root, not in the Devise user session, so sign-in events recorded before a user session exists are captured. The buffer is protected as part of the session through `SessionEncryptor::SENSITIVE_PATHS` (one KMS operation per save), not by a KMS call per event; its content and cap are unchanged by that choice. Buffering does not change `Tracker#track_event`'s return value and creates no `AgencyIdentity` at the service provider's agency. |
| **ATT-12** | Candidate agencies are the requested applications' recipients that are enrolled at authorize time; a request whose recipients are all unenrolled captures nothing. The buffer persists for the whole session and is delivered at most once per agency; an agency approved later in the session receives the full session history, and an agency re-approved receives a new consented event only. |
| **ATT-13** | The remembered path emits one `delegated-access-consented` (`remembered: true`) per authorization (per SP request id), not per session: the released-once guard is the SP request id recorded in the delegation context (`mark_released`, `released_for?`), so the handoff after a shown consent screen releases nothing and a later authorization in the same session releases again (D67). A revocation during the session ends the tokens but does not remove the recipient from live fan-out; it keeps receiving the session's events until the browser session ends. A later authorize request without delegation scopes leaves previously approved agencies receiving live events for the rest of the browser session. |
| **ATT-14** | `delegation_id` in events is the agency's earliest approved approval's id, the same choice the exchange and introspection make, so events and introspection agree; re-mapped copies and released historical `idv-*` events carry both `delegation_id` and `actor_issuer`. |
| **ATT-15** | Delegated historical release requires `historical_attempts_api_enabled` and a verified person, but not that the service provider's request be an identity-verification request: a delegation request is always identity-proofed, and the agency did not make the request. When the stored history is empty (remember-device or PIV/CAC sign-in) nothing is released and the once-only flag stays unset. |
| **ATT-16** | Every event schema under `docs/attempts-api/schemas/events` has a `TrackerEvents` method whose keyword parameters equal the event's properties (checked by `tracker_events_spec.rb` against the compiled bundle); the four delegated events have such methods and are written through them; the consented event carries `consented_at` (epoch seconds). |
| **ATT-17** | For delegated delivery, an agency counts as enrolled only if it is listed in `allowed_attempts_providers`, belongs to an agency, and a usable public key exists to encrypt events to (an explicit key in that configuration or the record's certificate): `ServiceProvider#attempts_api_deliverable?`. An agency listed without a usable key is treated as not enrolled and receives nothing; no error is raised and the person is not affected. Enrollment is the gate for delegated delivery (D74); the direct sign-in flow's behavior is unchanged. |
| **ATT-18** | Delegated events are delivered through the existing poll endpoint (`POST /api/attempts/poll`, RFC 8936) and inherit its acknowledge-to-drain contract: the endpoint deletes exactly the events whose keys the caller lists in `ack`, then returns the events that remain. Unacknowledged events stay available until `attempts_api_event_ttl_seconds` expires. A polling agency MUST acknowledge each event once it is durably stored so later polls return only new events; a caller that never acknowledges re-receives the whole retention window on every poll. |

## As built

1. Configuration: `token_exchange_attempts_delivery_enabled` (boolean, default `true`) in `lib/identity_config.rb` and `config/application.yml.default`; `DelegatedAccessEvents.enabled?` is that key with `token_exchange_enabled` and `attempts_api_enabled`. Per recipient: `ServiceProvider#attempts_api_deliverable?` and `#delegation_attempts_recipients` (each active API URL's `attempts_recipient`, de-duplicated, the application itself when none names one).
2. `AttemptsApi::DelegationContext` at the Rails session root (`delegation_context`, `delegated_attempts_buffer` = `BUFFER_KEY`; `MAX_BUFFERED_EVENTS = 200`; `mark_released`, `released_for?`), started by `OpenidConnect::AuthorizationController#start_delegation_context` when the validated request carries delegation scopes and the service provider is approved for delegation. While `active?`, `AttemptsApi::Tracker#track_event` copies each event into the buffer (`capture_for_delegation`, `user_uuid` and `google_analytics_cookies` removed) as plain `AttemptEvent#as_json` data; `delegated_attempts_buffer` is listed in `SessionEncryptor::SENSITIVE_PATHS` (`lib/session_encryptor.rb`) and events are rebuilt with `AttemptEvent.from_json` on read; `agency_uuid` skips `AgencyIdentity` creation for a buffer-only event; the buffer is discarded with the session.
3. `AttemptsApi::DelegatedRelease` at three points: `VerifySpAttributesConcern#record_delegation_consent`, `OpenidConnect::AuthorizationController#release_remembered_delegation` (before `login-completed` is recorded) and `Accounts::DelegatedAccess::ApprovalsController#create`. Per deliverable recipient it creates the `AgencyIdentity` if missing (`AgencyIdentityLinker.for(..., skip_create: false)`), forwards the buffer once per recipient per session (`buffer_delivered_to`), writes one `delegated-access-consented` per approval, releases the `idv-*` history under `AttemptsApi::HistoricalReleaseCheck` (shared with `Idv::HistoricalAttemptsConcern`, which keeps its own `idv_requested?` test; `Tracker.write_existing_user_events(extra_metadata:)` adds `delegation_id` and `actor_issuer`), and marks the recipient for live fan-out only when the current sign-in is to that service provider. A failing recipient is reported to error tracking and logged; the others continue; the person is never blocked.
4. `AttemptsApi::DelegatedEventWriter` includes `AttemptsApi::TrackerEvents` and implements `track_event`: `forward` re-maps a buffered or live copy (keeps event type, `jti`, timestamps, `subject.session_id`, IP address, user agent, device id, `application_url` and the event fields; replaces `user_uuid`; drops the analytics cookies; adds `delegation_id` and `actor_issuer`); a server-side event has `subject.session_id` null, `application_url: nil`, `language`, `aws_region`, no IP address, user agent, port or device, and `unique_session_id` as the SHA-1 hex digest the sign-in's events carry. Both encrypt to `recipient.attempts_public_key` and store through `AttemptsApi::RedisClient#write_event` under the recipient's issuer. Logged as `delegated_access_attempts_delivery(event_type, recipient_issuer, actor_issuer, success, event_count, skipped_reason, exception)`; `skipped_reason` is `delivery_disabled` or `recipient_not_enrolled`.
5. `DelegatedAccessEvents.token_issued` (from `OpenidConnectTokenExchangeForm#issue!` after the live entry is written), `.token_refreshed` (from `OpenidConnectRefreshTokenForm` after the live token is written) and `.access_revoked(grant:, reason:, resource_server:)` (from `TokenExchangeGrant#revoke!` and `TokenExchangeRefreshToken.revoke_family!`) call the generated `delegated_access_consented`, `delegated_access_token_issued`, `delegated_access_token_refreshed` and `delegated_access_revoked` methods. Token events carry `application`, `resource`, `scope`, `ial`, `aal`, `token_type` (`Bearer`, `DPoP` or `N_A`), `token_format` (`oauth` or `saml2`) and `expires_at`, never the token or anything derived from it; the revoked event carries `application`, `resource` (null when the whole approval ended) and `reason`.
6. Schemas: `docs/attempts-api/schemas/events/DelegationEvents.yml` (from `AllEvents.yml`), `events/delegated-access/DelegatedAccessConsented.yml`, `DelegatedAccessTokenIssued.yml`, `DelegatedAccessTokenRefreshed.yml`, `DelegatedAccessRevoked.yml`; `actor_issuer` and `delegation_id` in `shared/EventProperties.yml`, each described as present only on events delivered to an agency for a delegated session; the compiled bundle rebuilt. No `token-exchange-login-completed` event, schema or tracker method exists: it reported a sign-in at the agency, which never happens.
7. Migrations: none. Locale keys: none. Specs: `spec/services/attempts_api/{delegation_context,delegated_release,delegated_event_writer,historical_release_check,tracker}_spec.rb`, `spec/services/delegated_access_events_spec.rb`, `spec/controllers/openid_connect/authorization_controller_spec.rb`, `spec/controllers/sign_up/completions_controller_spec.rb`, `spec/controllers/accounts/delegated_access/approvals_controller_spec.rb`, the service provider, grant and refresh token model specs, `spec/requests/openid_connect/{token_exchange,token_refresh}_spec.rb`; 80 examples across the new and touched specs, the tracker events spec 44 examples; rubocop and the analytics-events lint clean.

## Decisions

- **D33** Reuse of a retired renewal credential ends the family and is reported as `refresh_token_reuse` to the one API's recipient (full entry in section 16).
- **D38** The fraud-signal plan stands in full: buffer, release at consent under the agency's identifier with the consented event and the history, live forwarding, token events with `delegation_id`.
- **D39** A remembered approval reused sends that sign-in's events and a consent event marked remembered, every time.
- **D58** The agency-role viewer that displays these events is the OAuth agency reference's.
- **D66** Account-page approvals release the consent event and history but start live fan-out only when the current sign-in is to that service provider.
- **D67** The released-once guard is keyed by the SP request id.
- **D74** Delivery is on by default and gated per recipient by Attempts API enrollment; the privacy-review items stay open.

## How to review

- Diff against `delegated-access-billing-reporting`.
- Check first: `Tracker#track_event` (return value and `should_track?` unchanged for the service provider's own event; no `AgencyIdentity` created for the buffer); the three release points and the once-only guards; that nothing names an application the person did not approve; the writer's two paths and what a server-side event omits; `SENSITIVE_PATHS` carries `delegated_attempts_buffer`. Run `make lint_tracker_events`.
- Specs that prove it: the four `spec/services/attempts_api/*` specs, `spec/services/delegated_access_events_spec.rb` (an unlisted agency, a listed agency without a key, and the switch off each receive nothing, without raising), the tracker spec, the authorization controller spec for the remembered path.
- Must not change for existing clients: existing event types and members are unchanged (FR-FRD-8); a service provider's direct Attempts delivery is byte for byte as before; an environment with no enrolled agency delivers nothing even with delivery on.
- Known open items, the privacy review's agenda (FR-FRD-8): the buffer carries the whole sign-in, failed-password events with the typed email included; the hashed `unique_session_id` correlates the service provider's and the agency's events; a mid-session revocation ends tokens but not live fan-out; the `idv-*` history is released whether or not the service provider's request was an identity-verification request (ATT-15); whether the Privacy Impact Assessment and SORN cover delivery on the basis of a delegation approval; whether Attempts enrollment should be a condition of accepting delegated access; how long an agency keeps delegated events and whether `delegation_id` is new personal data at the agency.
- Held until the harness run: `AttemptsApi::HistoricalRelease` and `EventDelivery` extracted from `DelegatedRelease`.

# 13. `delegated-access-config-content`: consent content and the delegation registry seeded from the configuration repository

**Stack position** 13 of 14, on `delegated-access-fraud-signals`. **Historical plan section** 5.17.

## Objective

The consent screen shows agency-written content: each agency's public name, logo, description and learn-more link, and each application's display name, what it lets the service do, what information it provides, whether it is read-only or can make changes, and where to learn more. The partner Dashboard, the long-term home for partner content, does not yet have these fields. This branch gives the content, the applications with their logos and API URLs, and the list of service providers approved for delegation one production path now: a YAML file, `delegated_access.yml`, maintained by the Login.gov team in the shared configuration repository `identity-idp-config`, reviewed by pull request and seeded by `rake db:seed` in every environment, exactly as `service_providers.yml` and `agencies.yml` already are (D77). The pull request review is the content review, and the approval is recorded in the file beside the content it approves. No admin interface is built in the identity provider: a new authenticated staff surface would need its own authorization, audit and security review and would compete with the configuration repository as a second source of truth.

Environment safety comes from the file, not from the seeder refusing to run: the file is keyed by Rails environment, each entry may carry `restrict_to_deploy_env` with the meaning service providers already give it, and every fictitious entry is restricted to the sandbox so production and staging write none of them. A missing file, or no section for the current environment, seeds nothing and raises nothing, which is what makes the order of merging the two repositories safe. Locally and in CI the same file shape is the fixture `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml`, whose `development` section remains the contract with the reference applications (section 10).

## Functional requirements

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **FR-ONB-3** | Owned by section 1. This branch delivers its content-maintenance amendment (D77): until the Dashboard gains the fields, the content, logos included, is maintained in `identity-idp-config` (`delegated_access.yml`) by the Login.gov team, reviewed by pull request and seeded in every environment, with the version pair set in that file. |
| **FR-ONB-5** | Owned by section 1. This branch delivers its amendment (D77): while content is maintained in `identity-idp-config`, the pull request review is the review, and the approval is recorded in the file (`consent_approved_at`, `consent_approved_by`) with the content it approves. |
| **FR-ONB-9** | Owned by section 1. This branch delivers its amendment (D77): the identity provider seeds the content with `rake db:seed` in every environment, production included; an entry's `restrict_to_deploy_env` decides where it is written, the fictitious entries are restricted to the sandbox, and the local fixture is the same file's development copy, linked in place by `bin/setup`. |

**FR-ONB-1**, **FR-ONB-2** and **FR-ONB-7** (section 1) are written through the same seeder and are unchanged in substance.

## Protocol rows

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **ONB-5** | Owned by section 1. This branch delivers its amendment (D77): the delegated-access content (agencies with their consent content, service providers approved for delegation, applications with their consent text, logos and API URLs) is managed through the same pipeline as every other service-provider record, with one more file, `delegated_access.yml` in `identity-idp-config`, linked to `config/delegated_access.yml` at deploy (`Deploy::Activate::FILES_TO_LINK`) and loaded by `DelegatedAccessSeeder` from `rake db:seed` in every environment; edits are pull requests in that repository; no admin interface is built. |
| **ONB-12** | Until the Dashboard gains the fields, the content source is `identity-idp-config`, not a branch seed file: the file is keyed by Rails environment (`development`, `production`), each section holding `agencies` (keyed by agency id, the shape of `agencies.yml`) and `service_providers` (keyed by issuer, the shape of `service_providers.yml`, nested `token_exchange_resource_servers` included). A missing file, or no section for the current environment, seeds nothing. The seeder has no environment refusal; `rake delegated_access:seed` remains as a convenience that runs the same seeder. Locally `bin/setup` links the fixture `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml` to `config/delegated_access.yml`, CI and the review-app image copy it into place, and its `development` section stays the contract with the reference applications (D77). |
| **ONB-15** | Every agency and service provider entry in `delegated_access.yml` MAY carry `restrict_to_deploy_env`, read through `DeployEnvRestriction` (shared with `ServiceProviderSeeder#write_service_provider?`) only when `RAILS_ENV=production`: `prod` is written in prod only, `staging` in staging only, `sandbox` in every other deployed environment, blank everywhere except prod. The key is stripped before writing because it is not a column. Every fictitious entry carries `sandbox`, in the configuration repository and in the fixture's `production` section, so prod and staging write none of them; the configuration repository's spec asserts it. An application's `logo` is a filename under `public/assets/images/sp-logos/` in `identity-idp-config` (never a path or URL), served from the identity provider's assets as every service-provider logo is; the spec asserts the file exists. |

## As built

1. `DelegatedAccessSeeder` (`app/services/delegated_access_seeder.rb`; `yaml_path:` for specs) reads `config/delegated_access.yml`: ERB, then `YAML.safe_load`, then `fetch(rails_env, {})`; a missing file or a dangling symlink (`Pathname#exist?`) seeds nothing and raises nothing. Entries under `agencies` are written when `restrict_to_deploy_env` allows, with that key stripped; entries under `service_providers` go through `ServiceProviderSeeder#write_service_provider`, so nested `token_exchange_resource_servers`, certificate lookup and the restriction behave as for every other service provider. An `active: false` entry deactivates the record at the next seed.
2. `DeployEnvRestriction` (`app/services/deploy_env_restriction.rb`) holds the rule `ServiceProviderSeeder#write_service_provider?` carried inline and that method delegates to it: read only when `RAILS_ENV=production`; `prod` in prod only, `staging` in staging only, `sandbox` in every deployed environment except prod and staging, blank everywhere except prod; in development and test every entry is written.
3. `db/seeds.rb` runs the seeder through `SeedRunner` after `AgencySeeder`; `delegated_access` is in `Deploy::Activate::FILES_TO_LINK` (`lib/deploy/activate.rb`), so the deploy links `<identity-idp-config checkout>/delegated_access.yml` to `config/delegated_access.yml` beside the other linked files. `bin/setup` links `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml` into place when `config/delegated_access.yml` is absent; `/config/delegated_access.yml` is in `.gitignore`; `.gitlab-ci.yml` and `dockerfiles/idp_review_app.Dockerfile` copy the fixture into place.
4. `lib/tasks/delegated_access.rake`: `rake delegated_access:seed` runs the same seeder on demand; its description names `config/delegated_access.yml` and says `db:seed` also runs it.
5. The fixture `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml` is keyed by Rails environment: the `development` section is the reference-application contract (section 10); the `production` section repeats the entries with `restrict_to_deploy_env: 'sandbox'` and hosts from `DELEGATION_SP_URL`, `DELEGATION_OIDC_AGENCY_URL` and `DELEGATION_SAML_AGENCY_URL`, so a personal sandbox seeds the hosted reference applications; API identifiers never change with the hostname. The header documents the keying, the restriction and both sections.
6. Comments on `AgencySeeder`, `ServiceProviderSeeder` and the registry migrations `20261009100000_add_delegation_content_to_agencies` and `20261009100300_add_delegation_service_provider_columns_to_service_providers` say the fields are loaded from the delegated-access configuration file by `DelegatedAccessSeeder` in every environment and that the Dashboard does not have them. Migrations: none new. Locale keys: none. Analytics events: none.
7. Companion repository `identity-idp-config`, branch `delegated-access`: `delegated_access.yml` (`development` and `production` sections, every `production` entry `sandbox`-restricted, the header explaining keying, restriction, logo filenames and hostname-independent identifiers); the three SVG logos `mybenefits-assistant.svg`, `housing-assistance-records.svg`, `retirement-benefits-portal.svg` under `public/assets/images/sp-logos/`; `spec/delegated_access_config_spec.rb` (8 examples on the file's shape: loads with `safe_load`, no duplicate keys, keyed by environment, both maps in every environment, every service provider has a `friendly_name` and an agency in the same environment, every application has a scope value and resource servers with an identifier and an `oauth` or `saml2` format, every logo exists, every `production` entry restricted to the sandbox); a "Delegated access content" README section.
8. Specs: `spec/services/delegated_access_seeder_spec.rb` (an absent file, a dangling symlink, a missing environment section, the `production` section in a sandbox and in prod and staging, `restrict_to_deploy_env` on an agency, idempotence, environment hosts, the reference-application contract), `spec/services/service_provider_seeder_spec.rb`, `spec/services/agency_seeder_spec.rb`, `spec/lib/deploy/activate_spec.rb`; 56 examples, 0 failures across the four; rubocop clean on the changed files; gitleaks clean on the commit range. Local check: with the configuration repository's file linked in place and `rake db:seed` run, the three applications' logos switch to the named SVGs and the identity provider serves `sp-logos/mybenefits-assistant.svg` with HTTP 200.

## Decisions

- **D77** Delegated-access content is edited as YAML in `identity-idp-config`, reviewed by pull request and seeded in every environment; no admin interface (full entry in section 16).
- **D10** The branch seed path stands as the local fixture; its environment refusal is replaced by per-entry restriction.
- **D51** One fixture file is the contract with the reference applications; its `development` section is unchanged by this branch.

## How to review

- Diff against `delegated-access-fraud-signals`.
- Check first: `DeployEnvRestriction` against the inline rule it replaces (the seeder spec and `service_provider_seeder_spec.rb` must agree); the seeder's handling of a missing file, a dangling symlink and `restrict_to_deploy_env`; that the `development` section of the fixture is unchanged (the harness contract); that no code path refuses an environment.
- Then read the configuration repository's `delegated_access.yml` and `spec/delegated_access_config_spec.rb` (8 examples): every `production` entry is `sandbox`-restricted, every logo exists, `logo` is a filename and never a path or URL.
- Specs that prove it: `spec/services/delegated_access_seeder_spec.rb` (56 examples with the other three), `spec/services/service_provider_seeder_spec.rb`, `spec/services/agency_seeder_spec.rb`, `spec/lib/deploy/activate_spec.rb`.
- Must not change for existing clients: `service_providers.yml` and `agencies.yml` seeding is unchanged; a deploy without `delegated_access.yml` on the configuration repository's `main` seeds nothing new and raises nothing; records already seeded are upserted idempotently.
- Deployment order: the deploy clones `identity-idp-config` at `main`, so the configuration repository's branch is not deployable until merged; either repository can go first because identity-idp `main` never reads the file and this seeder treats a missing file as nothing to seed.
- Known open items: the configuration repository's pre-existing "has no invalid logos" example in `spec/service_provider_config_spec.rb` fails until it also counts the logos `delegated_access.yml` references (left unchanged by the product owner for now); 24 Dependabot alerts there have a conservative fix on branch `update-deps` awaiting that repository's owners; the Dashboard remains the longer-term content path and `ServiceProviderUpdater` already writes the fields once it has them; held until the harness run: `DelegatedAccessSeeder` reusing `AgencySeeder#write_agency`, and the resource-server upsert shared by seeder and updater.

# 14. `delegated-access-encrypted-userinfo`: user-information responses encrypted to the service provider's registered certificate

**Stack position** 14 of 14, on `delegated-access-config-content`. **Historical plan section** 5.14.

## Objective

A confidential service provider that opts in on its record receives its user-information response as one encrypted object instead of plain JSON: the whole claims object the endpoint would have released for the granted scopes, document-metadata claims included, is returned as a single compact JWE (RFC 7516; RSA-OAEP-256 for key management, A256GCM for content) with content type `application/jwt`, per OpenID Connect Core 1.0 §5.3.2. The key is the public key of the record's first registered certificate, the one its `private_key_jwt` client assertions are verified against, so the partner decrypts with a key it already holds; nothing in the request names a key. The design exists for the passport agency's selfie and document data, which must be readable only by that agency and not by Login.gov's edge infrastructure, logs or any intermediary; encrypting the whole object rather than one field gives that end-to-end property with the simplest shape.

Only confidential clients can be served this way: a public client holds no registered key, and its per-session key-possession key is a signing key, not an encryption key. Every record that has not opted in receives the plain response unchanged, and delegated tokens remain refused at the endpoint regardless (FR-TOK-6; sections 6 and 8), so nothing changes for agencies reached by delegation. The endpoint fails closed: an opted-in record without a usable key is refused with a server error and the failure logged, never answered in the clear, and onboarding warns when it writes such a record. The feature serves one partner's need, so no fixture record opts in and no reference application decrypts; the public reference applications do not present it as a general feature (D81).

## Functional requirements

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **FR-DOS-12** | The selfie and document data released to the passport agency MUST be readable only by that agency and not by Login.gov's edge infrastructure, logs, the service provider or any intermediary. This is met at the user-information endpoint: a confidential service provider that opts in on its record (`userinfo_encrypted_response_alg`, `RSA-OAEP-256`) receives its whole user-information claims object as one JWE (RSA-OAEP-256, A256GCM) under `application/jwt`, encrypted to the public key of its registered certificate, the document-metadata claims of the document-images scope included, so the edge, logs and any intermediary see ciphertext; a record that opted in and has no usable key is refused with `server_error` and never answered in the clear; everyone else receives the plain response unchanged; delegated tokens remain refused. The claims are not also signed inside the encrypted response, the endpoint's TLS authenticating its origin as for the plain response; the whole object is encrypted, not the biometric alone; the image download endpoint of the direct document-images feature is unchanged. A separate encryption certificate and per-field encryption are documented for later (D81). |

Rows owned by other sections and relied on here: **FR-TOK-6** (delegated tokens are refused at the user-information endpoint; section 6), **INT-16** and **DOC-1** (document images and metadata are reachable only at the user-information endpoint with the service provider's own token; section 8 and section 15).

## Protocol rows

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **UINF-1** | A confidential service provider opts in with `service_providers.userinfo_encrypted_response_alg` (`RSA-OAEP-256` is the only value the model accepts; there is no `enc` column, A256GCM is fixed). `/api/openid_connect/userinfo` then returns the whole claims object `OpenidConnectUserInfoPresenter` released for the granted scopes, document-metadata claims under `document_images` included, as one compact JWE (RFC 7516; `alg` RSA-OAEP-256, `enc` A256GCM; the header carries only those two, no `kid`, no signature inside) with content type `application/jwt`, encrypted to the public key of the record's first registered certificate (`ssl_certs.first`), the one its `private_key_jwt` assertions verify against. The key comes from the token's own service provider record, never from the request. Every record that has not opted in receives the plain JSON response unchanged. A public client (`pkce`) has no usable key: it holds no registered certificate by definition and its key-possession key is a signing key, so `userinfo_encryption_key` is nil for it even with a certificate pasted on its record. Delegated tokens remain refused (EXC-6, EXC-18) (D81). |
| **UINF-2** | Fail closed: when an opted-in record has no usable RSA public key (no certificate, a certificate that cannot be read, or a public client), the endpoint answers HTTP 500 `{"error": "server_error", "error_description": …}` (locale `openid_connect.user_info.errors.encryption_unavailable`) with no claim in the body and logs `openid_connect_userinfo_encryption_failed` (`client_id`, `error`); it never falls back to plaintext. `ServiceProviderSeeder` and `ServiceProviderUpdater` log a warning (`ServiceProvider#warn_if_userinfo_encryption_unusable`) when they write such a record. `openid_connect_bearer_token` carries `encrypted` (nil when the token did not authenticate). |
| **UINF-3** | Discovery advertises `userinfo_encryption_alg_values_supported: ["RSA-OAEP-256"]` and `userinfo_encryption_enc_values_supported: ["A256GCM"]` unconditionally (OpenID Connect Discovery 1.0 §3); the members state the fixed algorithms, not whether any record has opted in. No reference application decrypts and no fixture record opts in; verification is by request specs that decrypt with the test certificate's key (D81). |

## As built

1. Migration `db/primary_migrate/20261010110000_add_userinfo_encrypted_response_alg_to_service_providers`: `service_providers.userinfo_encrypted_response_alg` (string, nullable; nil keeps the plain JSON response). The record opts in by naming the algorithm; partner configuration lives on the record, and the registry already holds the certificate the response is encrypted to.
2. `ServiceProvider::USERINFO_ENCRYPTED_RESPONSE_ALGS` (`%w[RSA-OAEP-256]`) with an inclusion validation (blank allowed); `#userinfo_encrypted_response?`; `#userinfo_encryption_key` (public key of `ssl_certs.first`; nil for a `pkce` record, a missing certificate or one that cannot be parsed); `#warn_if_userinfo_encryption_unusable` (a logged warning naming the issuer and the reason when an opt-in cannot be honored).
3. `OpenidConnect::UserInfoEncryptor` (`ALG = 'RSA-OAEP-256'`, `ENC = 'A256GCM'`, `NoUsableKeyError`): the JSON of the claims hash becomes the plaintext of one compact JWE whose header carries only `alg` and `enc`; a key that is not `OpenSSL::PKey::RSA` raises `NoUsableKeyError`.
4. `OpenidConnect::UserInfoController#show`: the presenter is unchanged; an opted-in record (`current_identity.service_provider_record`) is rendered as `application/jwt`, everyone else as JSON. `NoUsableKeyError` and `OpenSSL::OpenSSLError` become HTTP 500 `server_error` with the locale description and the analytics event `openid_connect_userinfo_encryption_failed` (`client_id`, `error`); no claim reaches that response. The bearer-token check is unchanged; `openid_connect_bearer_token` gains `encrypted`.
5. `OpenidConnectConfigurationPresenter`: `userinfo_encryption_alg_values_supported` (`[OpenidConnect::UserInfoEncryptor::ALG]`) and `userinfo_encryption_enc_values_supported` (`[OpenidConnect::UserInfoEncryptor::ENC]`), unconditional.
6. `ServiceProviderSeeder` and `ServiceProviderUpdater` call `warn_if_userinfo_encryption_unusable` after every write.
7. Locale key `openid_connect.user_info.errors.encryption_unavailable` in `config/locales/{en,es,fr,zh}.yml`. Analytics: `openid_connect_userinfo_encryption_failed` and the `encrypted` property of `openid_connect_bearer_token` in `app/services/analytics_events.rb`. Configuration keys: none. Fixture and reference applications: unchanged; no record opts in.
8. Specs: `spec/requests/openid_connect/user_info_encrypted_spec.rb` (12 examples: one compact JWE under `application/jwt` with nothing in the clear; `alg` and `enc` fixed with the header carrying only those two; decryption with the test certificate's key yields exactly the plain claims; another key cannot decrypt; a `DPoP` header on a bearer token is ignored as for the plain response; `encrypted: true` on the bearer-token event; the document-metadata claims under `document_images` travel inside the JWE and nowhere in the clear; a record that did not opt in receives the plain response exactly as before; no certificate, a certificate naming a missing file, and a public client with a certificate pasted on its record each answer 500 `server_error` with no claim in the body and log the failure; a delegated token is refused before any response is built); `spec/models/service_provider_spec.rb`, `spec/presenters/openid_connect_configuration_presenter_spec.rb`, `spec/services/service_provider_seeder_spec.rb`, `spec/services/service_provider_updater_spec.rb`, `spec/requests/openid_connect_userinfo_spec.rb` and `spec/controllers/openid_connect/user_info_controller_spec.rb` (the `encrypted: false` property). 273 examples, 0 failures across the touched specs; rubocop and the analytics lints clean; gitleaks clean on the commit range. Full suite as the top of the stack: 16,890 examples, 7 failures, the four pre-existing environment failures of section 10 plus three identity-verification browser specs that pass alone.

## Decisions

- **D50** The encrypted user-information response is the whole bundle as a JWE to the certificate registered for the token's own service provider, never a key named in the request (full entry in section 16).
- **D81** Built as one JWE to the opted-in confidential client's registered signing certificate, failing closed; no fixture opt-in and no decrypting reference application; a separate encryption certificate and per-field encryption documented, not built.

## How to review

- Diff against `delegated-access-config-content`.
- Start with the request spec: it decrypts the JWE with the test certificate's private key and compares the result with the plain response, checks the header carries only `alg` and `enc`, and asserts that no claim value appears in the clear, including the document-metadata claims under `document_images`.
- Then the fail-closed cases: no certificate, a certificate naming a file that does not exist, and a public client with a certificate pasted on its record each answer 500 `server_error` with no claim in the body and log `openid_connect_userinfo_encryption_failed`; confirm the controller has no plaintext fallback path.
- Then: a delegated token is still refused before any response is built (FR-TOK-6); a record that did not opt in receives the plain response exactly as before; discovery carries the two members with the fixed values in both switch states; the model validation refuses any algorithm other than `RSA-OAEP-256`.
- Specs that prove it: `spec/requests/openid_connect/user_info_encrypted_spec.rb` (12), `spec/models/service_provider_spec.rb`, `spec/presenters/openid_connect_configuration_presenter_spec.rb`, the seeder and updater specs for the warning.
- Must not change for existing clients: the plain response, byte for byte, for every record without the column; the bearer and key-possession checks of `AccessTokenVerifier`; the document-images download endpoint.
- Known open items: a separate registered encryption certificate, so the decrypting key is not the client-assertion signing key (key separation; documented, not built); per-field encryption (documented, not built, and constrained not to conflict with per-site keys, section 4, or the document-image attributes for specific relying parties, section 15); the end-to-end harness does not exercise the feature by design, so coverage is the request spec only.

# 15. The base branch and work outside this repository

Branch `login-delegated-access` is the base of the stack: the foundation with `main` merged (Appendix E), this document, `CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md` and the repository skill. One feature lives on the base itself rather than on a branch: the foundation's document-image sharing for a direct service provider (15.1). Four more parts of the work live outside this repository or are not code at all: third-party-initiated login in the reference applications (15.2), the reference applications and the end-to-end harness (15.3), the configuration repository (15.4), and two designs that are recorded and not built, the account-page history view (15.5) and delegated tokens at the user-information endpoint for agencies that cannot yet introspect (15.6). 15.7 records the enhancements documented for the encrypted user-information response, and 15.8 gives the current state of every functional requirement row no branch owns.

## 15.1 Document-image sharing for a direct service provider

### Objective

The Department of State wants fully online passport enrollment, which needs a fresh selfie captured under facial-match verification for manual adjudication; a stored selfie cannot be reused for it, and the biometric must never pass through the service provider the person signed in to. Login.gov meets the need in this phase with the foundation's document-images feature for a service provider the person signs in to directly, kept on the base and enabled only in the sandbox: the Department of State is registered there as a direct service provider on the document-images allow-list, the person gives a purpose-specific biometric consent, and the selfie and document data are released only at the user-information endpoint to that service provider's own sign-in (D49). Nothing obtained by delegation can reach an image: delegated tokens are refused at the user-information endpoint (FR-TOK-6), introspection and SAML assertions never carry document images or metadata (INT-16), and a delegated token never sets the consent that unlocks the artifacts. The STS still applies to any other State API the service provider uses.

The design that delivers the biometric to the agency through the agency verification channel is not built, and the recommendation is to decide it with the agency before any release beyond the sandbox: the SP 800-63-4 analysis under `docs/proofing/` on the base concludes that the feature should not be enabled in production before its remediations, and FR-DOS-15 stands as the open constraint on the artifact store. The encrypted user-information response (section 14) gives the released attributes their end-to-end property for a confidential service provider that opts in.

### Functional requirements

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **FR-DOS-1** | The Department of State case uses the STS for any State API the service provider calls on the person's behalf; all general STS requirements apply. The selfie and document data are not delivered through the STS in this phase: they are released only at Login.gov's user-information endpoint to the Department of State as a direct service provider on the document-images allow-list, with the purpose-specific biometric consent (D49). |
| **FR-DOS-2** | The biometric MUST NOT be included in the delegated token, in anything returned to the service provider, in a verification response, in a SAML assertion, or in any response to a party other than State. It is released only at the user-information endpoint to State's own direct sign-in; delegated tokens are refused at that endpoint (FR-TOK-6), so a delegated token can never fetch it (D49). |
| **FR-DOS-3** | Because a delegated token never carries higher assurance than the sign-in it came from (FR-FIT-5), the person's sign-in to the service provider MUST itself include the facial-match verification State requires whenever State's passport API is requested. Login.gov MUST perform that verification during the sign-in, before consent, rather than relying on an earlier verification. |
| **FR-DOS-4** | State's passport API MUST be registered as its own agency API whose consent content states, in State's words, that approving the Department of State releases the fresh selfie to State for passport adjudication. |
| **FR-DOS-5** | When a sign-in requests State's passport API, Login.gov MUST capture a fresh selfie in that sign-in, even for a person who has already verified their identity with Login.gov. A selfie captured in an earlier verification MUST NOT be reused for State. |
| **FR-DOS-6** | Login.gov MUST tell the person, before capture, that the selfie will be released to the Department of State for passport adjudication, who will see it, how long each party keeps it, and that declining means they cannot continue this process online. This notice MUST be specific to the biometric and MUST appear in addition to the consent screen. |
| **FR-DOS-7** | If the person declines the Department of State on the consent screen, the selfie MUST NOT be released and the rest of the sign-in MUST proceed normally. |
| **FR-DOS-8** | Capture MUST meet the same liveness and quality standards as Login.gov's existing facial-match verification, and MUST meet Login.gov's accessibility requirements, including an alternative path for people who cannot complete selfie capture. |
| **FR-DOS-9** | Login.gov MUST record the capture, the consent, and each release as auditable events. |
| **FR-DOS-10** | The selfie is released only at the user-information endpoint, to the Department of State's registered direct service provider on the document-images allow-list, with the document-images scope and the person's biometric consent, and never through delegation (D49). |
| **FR-DOS-11** | The selfie MUST be released only if State's Login.gov agreement includes it as an attribute State is entitled to receive (FR-VER-4). No other agency agreement MAY include it, and the service provider's own agreement MUST NOT. |
| **FR-DOS-12** | Owned by section 14 (encrypted user-information responses). |
| **FR-DOS-13** | The release MUST include enough context for adjudication: when the selfie was captured, the verification outcome, the assurance level, the person's identifier for State, and the delegation identifier. |
| **FR-DOS-14** | The selfie MUST be available for release only while the person's sign-in to the service provider is active (FR-VER-5). After the sign-in ends, verification MUST still confirm the token but MUST NOT include the selfie, and MUST say so. |
| **FR-DOS-15** | Login.gov MUST NOT retain the selfie beyond its existing identity-verification retention rules because of this release, and MUST NOT create a new store of biometric images for the purpose of release. The direct feature kept on the base has an artifact store and is enabled in the sandbox only; this row is the open constraint for any wider release (D49). |
| **FR-DOS-16** | State MUST be able to decrypt, verify, and view the image with tooling it controls; Login.gov SHOULD provide a reference decryption and verification example in the reference agency API (FR-OPS-5). |
| **FR-DOS-17** | Every release MUST be logged with the person's identifier for State, the delegation identifier, the time, and the key used, without logging the image. |
| **FR-DOS-18** | Fraud-signal delivery (section 12) and billing (section 11) for State MUST work exactly as for any other agency API; the biometric adds no exception. |
| **FR-DOS-19** | If State chooses to capture the selfie through its own identity-proofing vendor rather than receive it from Login.gov, FR-DOS-5 to FR-DOS-17 do not apply. The STS still applies to any other State API the service provider uses, and Login.gov's role in the passport case is limited to ordinary sign-in and identity verification of the person. |

FR-TOK-6 (section 8), FR-VER-4 and FR-VER-5 (section 8) govern what a delegated token and introspection can release.

### Protocol rows

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **DOC-1** | The direct-service-provider document-images feature (`document_images` scope, `document_images_sharing_service_providers` allow-list, biometric consent checkbox on the completions screen, `document_artifacts` store, download endpoint, 90-day expiry job) is kept as built and is enabled (`document_images_sharing_enabled: true`) in the sandbox only; it is off in every other environment. The Department of State is registered there as a direct service provider on the allow-list. No delegated channel for images is built. |

INT-16 (section 8) states that document images and metadata are never released by introspection or in a SAML assertion.

### As kept on the base

1. `document_images` scope, honored for service providers listed in `document_images_sharing_service_providers`, behind `document_images_sharing_enabled`; both keys keep their `false` and `[]` defaults and are set only in the sandbox (Appendix C).
2. The biometric consent checkbox on the completions screen, purpose-specific, re-asked after re-proofing; the person's consent is recorded as `biometric_sharing_consent_at` on the identity for that service provider, which a delegated token never sets.
3. `document_artifacts` and `document_metadata`: encrypted rows linked to the profile through the capture session, with row locking against the worker race; `ExpireDocumentArtifactsJob` removes artifacts after 90 days.
4. `GET /api/openid_connect/document_images/:type`: download authenticated by the service provider's own access token (and by a DPoP proof for a bound client, section 5); the document number and dates are read from the user-information response. Releases are audited and never log the image (FR-DOS-9, FR-DOS-17).
5. The mDL exemption: `SocureImageRetrievalJob` persists no artifacts for mDL sessions.
6. Two design documents under `docs/proofing/`, including the SP 800-63-4 analysis that is the precondition for any release beyond the sandbox.
7. The Department of State's direct-service-provider record in `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml` and in the configuration repository's file (sandbox-restricted), so the sandbox can exercise the feature.
8. Differences from the rows above that remain open with the agency: the feature ships the document front, back or passport image and the document number and dates as well as the selfie; declining the biometric consent blocks the person instead of withholding the image and continuing (FR-DOS-7); the download endpoint serves the image over TLS, while the end-to-end encryption of FR-DOS-12 is the encrypted user-information response of section 14, which covers the metadata claims and not the download endpoint.

### Decisions

- **D49** Document images stay a direct-service-provider feature, at the user-information endpoint only, enabled in the sandbox only.
- **D50** Encrypted user-information responses planned as the end-to-end encryption route; built under D81 (section 14).
- **D83** Delegated tokens stay refused at the user-information endpoint.

## 15.2 Third-party-initiated login (the alternative pattern)

### Objective

A service provider can send a signed-in person to an agency's own service, the agency signs the person in through Login.gov without asking for credentials again, and the person returns to the service provider, with no delegated token involved (OpenID Connect Core 1.0 §4). The pattern fits when the agency must interact with the person directly in its own service, require its own assurance level or run its own workflow with the person present; the STS fits when the service provider calls agency APIs from the person's browser with its own tokens, combines agencies, continues through refresh while the person is away from the agency, or passes attributes the service provider itself must not see. A business case may use both. The Department of State case uses the STS.

Three things do not change under this pattern. Billing: the agency's sign-in is a direct sign-in to the agency and is billed to the agency exactly as one; the initiating service provider is not billed for it. Consent: the person sees Login.gov's existing consent screen for the agency's own attributes; nothing is delegated, so the delegated-access consent (sections 2 and 3) is not involved. Fraud signals: the agency receives the Attempts API events of a direct sign-in, and both applications log the hand-off (start, return, status) with a correlation hint that carries no identity.

Login.gov itself needs no change: the pattern runs against the unchanged identity provider and is implemented in the three reference applications so partners can see both patterns side by side. It can be exercised in the sandbox as soon as the agency reference applications are registered there as ordinary service providers. One operational note: with all reference applications on one host, each must use its own session cookie name, since browsers scope cookies by host and not port.

### Functional requirements

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **FR-TPL-1** | The pattern MUST follow OpenID Connect Core 1.0 section 4 (Initiating Login from a Third Party): the service provider sends the person's browser to the agency's published login-initiation address, identifying Login.gov as the identity provider and the page the person should land on. |
| **FR-TPL-2** | The agency MUST sign the person in through its own Login.gov integration, with its own assurance requirements, attribute agreement, consent screen, fraud-signal enrollment, and billing. Login.gov MUST treat this sign-in exactly as any direct sign-in to the agency. |
| **FR-TPL-3** | When the person has an active Login.gov session, Login.gov MUST NOT ask for credentials again unless the agency requires it or a higher assurance level requires further verification. |
| **FR-TPL-4** | The agency MUST refuse an initiation request unless `iss` names the Login.gov it is configured to use, and unless `target_link_uri` matches an allow-list of exact origins the agency approved (the two checks OpenID Connect Core §4 requires of the relying party: a forged `iss` would send the person to an attacker's identity provider, and an unchecked `target_link_uri` is an open redirect). |
| **FR-TPL-5** | The service provider MUST send `iss` (Login.gov), `login_hint` and `target_link_uri`. `login_hint` MUST be a correlation identifier the service provider generates for this hand-off (a UUID), recorded on its side, single-use, and short-lived (ten minutes in the reference). It MUST carry no identity. The agency MUST remember it for the sign-in it starts and MUST treat the Login.gov sign-in as the only proof of who the person is. When the service provider needs the agency to trust task context or a return address beyond the allow-listed origin, it SHOULD add a signed, single-use statement; the correlation hint alone is sufficient for the flow to be correct. |
| **FR-TPL-6** | When its sign-in completes, the agency MUST send the person to `target_link_uri` with the same `login_hint`, its own identifier as `iss` (client_id or SAML entityID), and `status` (`signed_in` or `failed`), and MUST clear the hand-off so it cannot be replayed. The service provider MUST accept a return only when `login_hint` matches a hand-off it issued and still has outstanding in the same browser session, so a result cannot be attached to a different person's session; `iss` and `status` from an unmatched return MUST be ignored. The return MUST carry no identity data. |
| **FR-TPL-7** | The person MUST be told, at the hand-off, that they are leaving the service provider and which agency they are going to, and MUST be able to return to the service provider when done. |
| **FR-TPL-8** | Nothing in this pattern MAY require the service provider to hold a token for the agency or to call the agency's systems directly. |
| **FR-TPL-9** | A SAML agency MUST complete the return from a page of its own (an immediate refresh plus a visible link) rather than an HTTP redirect from its assertion consumer. Login.gov's SAML POST-binding page carries a Content-Security-Policy `form-action` limited to Login.gov and the agency's consumer URL, and Chrome applies `form-action` to the redirects that follow a form submission, so a cross-origin redirect from the consumer is blocked and the person is left on Login.gov's page. An OIDC agency's callback is reached by a GET redirect and MAY redirect directly. |
| **FR-TPL-10** | The agency's sign-in MUST be billed to the agency as a direct sign-in and MUST deliver the agency's ordinary Attempts API events; the service provider MUST NOT be billed for it. Both applications MUST log the hand-off's start, return and status with the correlation hint and no identity data. |

### Protocol rows

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **TPL-1** | The reference service provider MUST start the flow with a browser redirect to the agency's `initiate_login_uri` carrying `iss` = Login.gov's issuer URL, `login_hint` = a UUID the service provider generates for this hand-off, and `target_link_uri` = the service provider's own return URL (`/third_party/return`). |
| **TPL-2** | `login_hint` is a correlation identifier only. It MUST carry no identity, MUST be single-use, MUST expire (ten minutes in the reference), and the service provider MUST accept a return only when the hint matches an outstanding hand-off it recorded; the browser public client keeps that record in the browser's session storage, since it has no server session (D57). The agency learns who the person is only from its own Login.gov sign-in; neither party treats the hint as identity. |
| **TPL-3** | Each agency reference app MUST expose `GET /initiate_login`, MUST refuse the request unless `iss` equals the Login.gov issuer it is configured to use, and MUST refuse unless `target_link_uri` matches an allow-list of exact origins. No wildcards; `https` or the explicit local-development origins only. |
| **TPL-4** | On a valid initiation the agency MUST remember `login_hint` and the validated `target_link_uri` in its session and start its ordinary Login.gov sign-in: the OIDC agency its usual authorization code request, the SAML agency its usual AuthnRequest, with the scopes, attributes and ACR it uses for a direct sign-in. The hint is not forwarded to Login.gov. |
| **TPL-5** | When the agency's sign-in completes it MUST send the person to the remembered `target_link_uri` with `login_hint` (the same UUID), `iss` (the agency's own client_id or SAML entityID) and `status` (`signed_in` or `failed`), then clear the hand-off so it cannot be replayed. An OIDC agency MAY do this with an HTTP redirect from its callback. A SAML agency MUST NOT redirect to the third party straight from its assertion consumer, because of the `form-action` policy on Login.gov's SAML POST-binding page (FR-TPL-9); it answers the consumer POST with its own page that continues to `target_link_uri` (an immediate refresh plus a visible link). |
| **TPL-6** | The agency MUST show on its signed-in page that the session was started by a third party, and MUST NOT include any identity data in the return redirect; what the service provider learns is only that the hand-off it started completed. |
| **TPL-7** | The pattern is browser-only. The service provider MUST NOT hold a token for the agency, MUST NOT call the agency's systems directly, and the agency's Attempts API events, billing and consent are those of a direct sign-in. The reference apps MUST state this difference from delegated access in their user interface and documentation. |

The reference implementation uses the plain §4 parameters, with the two MUST-verify rules (TPL-3) and the single-use hint (TPL-2) as the controls; a signed hand-off statement carrying task context remains an option for a production integration (FR-TPL-5).

### Where it lives

| Repository | Implementation |
|---|---|
| `identity-sts-sinatra` (the browser public client) | Page code: the hint, the initiation URL, the hand-off record and its match in session storage; `/third_party/start` and `/third_party/return` as page states; a dashboard section; `THIRD_PARTY_LOGIN_TARGETS` and `THIRD_PARTY_RETURN_URI` in the non-secret JSON configuration; documentation. |
| `identity-oidc-sinatra` (OIDC agency) | `GET /initiate_login` with the `iss` and allow-list checks, the hand-off stored in session, the redirect after `/auth/result`, `THIRD_PARTY_TARGET_LINK_ALLOWLIST`. |
| `identity-saml-sinatra` (SAML agency) | The same, except that `POST /consume` answers with the agency's own return page (refresh plus link) instead of a redirect. |
| `identity-idp` | None. |

### Decisions

- **D57** The service provider reference application is a standards-based browser public client, so the initiator is page code.

## 15.3 The reference applications and the end-to-end harness

### Objective

Everything in sections 1 to 14 puts new obligations on partners: a service provider must exchange with a proof, rotate refresh tokens and revoke; an agency API must introspect, enforce scope, verify proofs and join Attempts events on `delegation_id`. Without working examples each partner would interpret the standards differently and Login.gov would debug the same mistakes agency by agency. Three reference applications and a live end-to-end harness are therefore part of the capability: they are how the design is validated with the first partner agencies, how later agencies onboard without a support engagement, and the acceptance test for every branch from the exchange onward.

The three repositories and the branches that carry the work: the service provider reference `GSA-TTS/identity-sts-sinatra`, branch `main`, a browser public client; the OIDC agency reference `18F/identity-oidc-sinatra` and the SAML agency reference `18F/identity-saml-sinatra`, branch `login-delegated-access`. The contract between them and this repository is the `development` section of `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml` (one delegation scope per application, issuers, resource identifiers, redirect URIs, hostnames) plus the harness; the harness reads issuers, identifiers, scope values and hostnames from that file and from nowhere else (D51). One side is changed only together with the other.

### Protocol rows

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **REF-IMPL-1** | The reference service provider MUST request `token_exchange:*` scopes, display what the person approved, perform the exchange from the browser with a DPoP proof, call an agency API with the delegated token, refresh on a timer with rotation, and revoke on demand (D57). |
| **REF-IMPL-2** | A reference resource server MUST be provided that accepts delegated tokens, introspects them with its own `private_key_jwt` assertion, enforces `scope` per route, and shows every decision it made. As built the roles are separate repositories (one service provider, one OIDC resource server, one SAML resource server) rather than one role-switched application. |
| **REF-IMPL-3** | The OIDC resource server reference (`identity-oidc-sinatra`) MUST include an Attempts API viewer in the agency role that polls with the agency's credentials and joins events to API calls on `delegation_id`; the SAML agency's events are delivered by Login.gov and can be observed with that viewer configured for the SAML agency's credentials (D58). |
| **REF-IMPL-4** | All roles MUST run against a local identity provider with a single command, using the fixture `config/delegated_access.localdev.yml` shipped in `identity-idp`, and against the sandbox by changing environment variables only (the sandbox seeds the same file with hostnames from `DELEGATION_SP_URL`, `DELEGATION_OIDC_AGENCY_URL` and `DELEGATION_SAML_AGENCY_URL`). |
| **REF-IMPL-5** | An automated end-to-end test MUST drive the full flow (sign in, consent with remember on and off, exchange, API call, introspection, refresh, reuse detection, revocation, Attempts delivery) and MUST run in CI against a local identity provider. |
| **REF-IMPL-6** | The repositories MUST include a partner-facing guide: a sequence diagram, the exact requests and responses, the proof and assertion payloads, error handling, and a checklist for each role derived from the exchange, introspection and refresh rows, each item linking to the reference function and the RFC section. |
| **REF-IMPL-7** | Reference code MUST be written so that partners can copy it: one function per protocol step, standards cited in comments, no framework-specific cleverness. |
| **REF-IMPL-8** | The reference service provider keeps no token on a server: tokens live in page memory for the duration of the sign-in and the DPoP key, generated non-extractable, never leaves the browser, so a stolen token is unusable without it (D57). |
| **REF-IMPL-9** | The end-to-end harness MUST be skipped automatically when no identity provider is configured (`E2E_IDP_URL` unset) so unit CI passes without a stack; scenarios that need a wait or a token read from the browser MUST be gated behind explicit environment variables and marked pending otherwise. |
| **REF-IMPL-10** | The Attempts API viewer in `identity-oidc-sinatra` MUST decode unsigned events as JSON as well as signed ones, and MUST poll once per page load, without acknowledging, so an operator refreshing the page keeps seeing the last hour of activity; a production agency acknowledges to advance the pointer (ATT-18). |
| **REF-IMPL-11** | Anything a reference resource server echoes back to the service provider from an introspection response MUST be filtered to the token members (`active`, `aud`, `scope`, `act`, `client_id`, `acr`, `iat`, `exp`, `token_type`, `delegation_id`, `session_live`). Identity claims MUST be consumed by the API exactly as user-information claims are, with the same SSN redaction, and MUST never be echoed unredacted to the service provider. |
| **REF-IMPL-12** | The reference service provider MUST treat a SAML assertion as single-use: it presents a given assertion to the agency API at most once and refreshes before any further call, unless the agency has declared that it accepts reuse (SAML-10). |
| **RS-DPOP-1** | A resource server whose tokens may be key-bound MUST verify the DPoP proof on every request per RFC 9449 §4.3, using `cnf.jkt` from introspection, and MUST refuse a bound token presented with the `Bearer` scheme. Whether a service provider's tokens are bound follows its record's `dpop_required` (D82); there is no per-API setting. Each agency confirms this verification before rollout. |

### As built

1. **The browser public client** (`identity-sts-sinatra`, `main`): PKCE; a DPoP key generated non-extractable with WebCrypto and held as an IndexedDB `CryptoKey`, a new key per sign-in; `dpop_jkt` on the authorization request; the authorization-code exchange, the token exchange, refresh, revocation, its own introspection and the agency API calls made from the browser, each with a proof (`htm`, `htu`, `iat`, `jti`, `ath`), including the `DPoP-Nonce` retry of RFC 9449 §8 although Login.gov issues no nonces. The server is static hosting plus a non-secret `/config.json` (issuer, `client_id`, redirect URI, agency API origins and resource identifiers, delegation scopes, third-party targets); it holds no private key, shared secret or token. Its `.gitleaks.toml` allow-lists the `PHNhbWw-assertion` placeholder (D76). Its service-provider record sets `pkce: true`, `token_exchange_enabled_sp: true` and `dpop_required: true`.
2. **The agency references** (`identity-oidc-sinatra`, `identity-saml-sinatra`, `login-delegated-access`): introspection with `private_key_jwt`, scope enforcement per route, DPoP proof verification against `cnf.jkt`, refusal of a bound token under `Bearer`, a decision log, fail-closed 503 when introspection errors or times out; CORS for the service provider's origin (`Authorization`, `DPoP` and `Content-Type` allowed, `WWW-Authenticate` exposed so the browser can read a challenge, D59); the SAML agency refuses a second presentation of one assertion; the OIDC agency's Attempts viewer with a "Delegated sessions" join on `delegation_id`; `GET /initiate_login` for the alternative pattern (15.2).
3. **The harness** (`spec/e2e/` in `identity-sts-sinatra`, Capybara and Selenium, host networking in the compose file so every URL matches the local contract) drives the page through sign-in, consent, exchange, API call, refresh and revocation and reads tokens and the DPoP thumbprint from the page. Scenarios: approve both applications with remember off, exchange each and call both APIs; approve one, call the other and receive `insufficient_scope`, exchange for it and receive `consent_required`; approve none and receive no `token_exchange:*` scope; remember on, the second authorization skips consent and the agency receives `delegated-access-consented` with `remembered: true`; a second authorization adding an application shows the screen with the approved one already recorded; refresh and the old token inactive after expiry (gated behind `E2E_WAIT_FOR_EXPIRY=true`); replay of a rotated refresh token, `invalid_grant`, the family revoked, the API call refused and `delegated-access-revoked` with `reason: refresh_token_reuse`; revocation from Account → Delegated access with the API call refused at once; the wrong resource server introspecting and receiving `active: false` (a request spec against the agency references); the agency viewer showing the sign-in, MFA, consent and token-issued events under one `delegation_id` and the other agency's viewer showing nothing; billing as a request spec in `identity-idp` (one `sp_return_logs` row per exchange with `access_type: delegated`, none on refresh, one user for direct plus delegated in a month). The harness is skipped unless `E2E_IDP_URL` is set.
4. **Running it.** The harness signs in as an identity-verified development user (`E2E_USER_EMAIL`; the primed `test2@test.com`, not the unverified default) or every scenario stops at `/verify/welcome`; the identity provider runs with `LAUNCHY_DRY_RUN=true` so development emails are written to `tmp/letter_opener/` and not opened; each scenario empties the Attempts store (`attempts-api-events:*`) first because the viewers poll without acknowledging; WebMock is disabled for the live run; the browser harness drives every page a real user can meet (rules of use, the second-MFA reminder, the first-visit authorization confirmation, the RP-initiated logout confirmation) and finds USWDS checkboxes with `visible: :all`; servers are restarted after pulling commits and the identity provider re-seeded after a fixture certificate is added. Browser specs in `identity-idp` route through the capybara-webmock proxy on port 9292, which the reference service provider also uses, so they run with `Capybara::Webmock.port_number` set to a free port (Appendix C).
5. **Not demonstrated by design.** No reference application decrypts user-information responses and no fixture record opts in (section 14, D81): the feature serves one partner's need and the public references must not present it as general. The reference agency never calls the user-information endpoint with a delegated token.
6. **The partner guide** (REF-IMPL-6; FR-OPS-6) lives with the reference applications: the sequence diagram with the file and function behind each arrow, the service-provider and resource-server checklists, every request and response from the harness with placeholders including every error case, and the sandbox onboarding steps (what to send Login.gov and what to expect back).

### Decisions

- **D51** One fixture is the contract between the seeder and the harness.
- **D57** The service provider reference is a browser public client; no confidential-client reference and no server-side proof signing.
- **D58** Attempts API code removed from the two references that did not have it upstream.
- **D59** CORS for browser calls at the agencies and at the identity provider's revocation and introspection endpoints.
- **D76** gitleaks allowlists in both repositories.

## 15.4 The configuration repository

Production content for delegated access is configuration, not an admin interface (D77): the agencies' consent content, the service providers approved for delegation and the agency applications with their consent text, logos and API URLs are maintained by the Login.gov team as `delegated_access.yml` in `18F/identity-idp-config`, reviewed by pull request and seeded by `rake db:seed` in every environment, exactly as `service_providers.yml` and `agencies.yml` are. Section 13 describes the seeder; this subsection records the repository side.

1. Branch `delegated-access` of `identity-idp-config` carries `delegated_access.yml` (keyed by Rails environment with `development` and `production` sections; the header explains the keying, `restrict_to_deploy_env`, that `logo` is a filename under `public/assets/images/sp-logos/` and never a path or URL, and that resource identifiers do not change with the hostname), the three fictitious logos, `spec/delegated_access_config_spec.rb` (8 examples on the file's shape: `safe_load`, no duplicate keys, keyed by environment, both maps in every environment, every service provider with a `friendly_name` and an agency in the same environment, every application with a scope value and resource servers with an identifier and an `oauth` or `saml2` format, every logo present, every `production` entry restricted to the sandbox) and a "Delegated access content" section in the README. Every `production` entry is `restrict_to_deploy_env: 'sandbox'`, so `prod` and `staging` write none of the fictitious content. The reference browser client's entry sets `dpop_required: true`.
2. Deploy clones the configuration repository at `main` (`Deploy::Activate#clone_idp_config`), so the branch is not deployable until merged. Merging it ahead of this stack is safe because identity-idp `main` neither links nor reads the file; deploying the stack first is safe because the seeder treats a missing file as nothing to seed.
3. Open items in that repository, with owners, are in Appendix D (items 13 and 24): the pre-existing "has no invalid logos" example, the Dependabot update branch, and the Dashboard fields that would eventually let partners edit this content themselves.

## 15.5 Delegated-access history on the account page (planned, not built)

Account → Delegated access shows remembered approvals in their current state only, with an end-all action (D54); past changes are visible through the account history events and emails that approvals and revocations write (D87). The planned follow-up, decided and not built (D55), is a per-service-provider history on that page: past approvals with their source (consent screen or account page) and whether they were remembered; revocations with their reason (the person, service provider disconnect, material content change, account suspension or deletion, refresh token reuse); and token issuance and refresh events per application, each with its date. It is sourced from the grant rows, which are never deleted, and from the token issuance record (section 7, D26 and D79), not from a new table; the page shows 12 months, after which rows are still kept for billing and audit but no longer shown. It depends on section 3 (the page) and section 7 (the issuance record).

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **FR-CUX-15** | The page SHOULD offer, per service provider, a history of past approvals (with source and whether remembered), revocations (with reason: the person, service provider disconnect, material content change, account suspension or deletion, refresh token reuse) and token issuance and renewal events, each with its date, covering the last 12 months, built from the approval records and the token issuance record rather than a new store. Not built; design recorded here (D55). |

ACC-7 (the protocol row for the same view) is satisfied by the same design when built.

## 15.6 Delegated tokens at the user-information endpoint for agencies without introspection (documented, not built)

**What stands.** Agencies verify delegated tokens by introspection (RFC 7662), authenticated with their own key (`private_key_jwt`); the user-information endpoint refuses a delegated token with or without a proof (FR-TOK-6; EXC-6, EXC-18; INT-1). `AccessTokenVerifier#load_identity` resolves a token against `identities` alone and delegated tokens live in `DelegatedTokenStore`, so none is ever found there; the comment on that method says not to add a second lookup path. The product owner confirmed this (D83).

**Why a design is recorded.** Agencies do not support the introspection endpoint today and need time to add it. Login.gov may need to let an agency present a delegated token at the user-information endpoint as a bearer token during a transition, then withdraw the opening. The design is recorded so that, if the need arrives, it can be built with the context intact rather than improvised; the recommendation is to hold it until an agency asks and cannot implement introspection in time, and then to build only this.

**Why it is not built, stated plainly.** The user-information endpoint is authenticated by nothing but the token, so whoever holds a delegated token would receive the person's attributes. The agency cannot present a DPoP proof for a delegated token, because the token is bound to the service provider's browser key and not to any key the agency holds, so acceptance there is bearer acceptance: a key-bound delegated token, useless without the key at the agency's API, would be usable without it at the user-information endpoint; and an unflagged public client's delegated tokens are already bearer tokens (D82). The attribute bundle would leave Login.gov to an unauthenticated caller, where introspection releases it only to the registered agency for that API. SAML delegated tokens are assertions and have no meaning at the user-information endpoint. The reference agency never calls it with a delegated token. Introspection is the strongest control in the design and no agency has asked for the opening.

| Id | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **INT-17** | Documented, not built (D83). (a) Opt-in per agency API on the registry record: `token_exchange_resource_servers.userinfo_allowed` (boolean, default false) with a mandatory `userinfo_allowed_until` date, set through `identity-idp-config` and through the Dashboard once it has the fields (D7), never through a request parameter; `oauth` APIs only, since a `saml2` API is issued assertions. The opening lapses on its own at the date. (b) At the user-information endpoint, a bearer token that resolves to no `identities` row but to a live `DelegatedTokenStore` entry whose resource server has the opening in force receives, as the user-information JSON, exactly what introspection releases to that agency, built by `DelegatedTokenClaims`: `sub` as the agency's pairwise identifier, `act` naming the service provider, the application's attribute bundle while the person's sign-in is live and identifiers and email afterwards with `session_live`, and `aud` and `delegation_id`. In every other case the refusal is as today, with or without a proof. (c) The entry must be live, unrevoked and unexpired by the one Redis read, and held to the conditions introspection applies (`OpenidConnectIntrospectForm#token_active?`: the person present and not suspended, the grant `valid_now?`, the resource server `usable?`, the service provider still approved), so revocation keeps propagating. (d) `openid_connect_bearer_token` gains `delegated: true` and the resource server identifier; an alert on any such use is recommended. (e) The encrypted user-information response (section 14) does not apply: the caller is the agency, not the service provider the token's identity belongs to, and an agency with a registered certificate could as well implement introspection with the same key; no encryption variant is designed. (f) `ServiceProviderSeeder` and `ServiceProviderUpdater` warn when `userinfo_allowed` is set without a date or with a date already past; such a record has no opening.
